export type DemoFrame =
  | { kind: 'pick'; words: string[]; picks: number[]; chip: string }
  | { kind: 'type'; lines: { label?: string; text: string }[] }
  | { kind: 'place'; left: string; right: string }
  | { kind: 'choose'; question: string; options: string[]; correct: number };

export type DemoStep = { title: string; caption: string; frame: DemoFrame };
export type DrillDemo = { sentence?: string; steps: DemoStep[] };

const words = (sentence: string) => sentence.split(' ');

    { title: 'Find the indicators', caption: 'Click each indicator word, then choose its group.', frame: { kind: 'pick', words: words(sentence), picks, chip } },
    { title: 'Keep the complete ideas', caption: 'Click the first and last word of each condition. Leave the indicator out.', frame: { kind: 'pick', words: words(sentence), picks: words(sentence).map((_, i) => i).filter(i => !picks.includes(i)), chip: 'Both co
    { title: 'Define your symbols', caption: 'Give each condition its own letter, and reuse it.', frame: { kind: 'type', lines: [{ text: `${s1} = ${one.toLowerCase()}` }, { text: `${s2} = ${two.toLowerCase()}` }] } },
    { title: 'Build the diagram', caption: 'Put your letters in the slots: what is enough on the left, what it requires on the right.', frame: { kind: 'type', lines: [{ label: 'Your diagram', text: `${s1} → ${s2}` }] } },
    { title: 'Reverse and negate', caption: 'Switch the sides, then turn NOT on for both.', frame: { kind: 'type', lines: [{ label: 'Original', text: `${s1} → ${s2}` }, { label: 'Contrapositive', text: `NOT ${s2} → NOT ${s1}` }] } },
    { title: 'Read the original', caption: 'Write the original rule as an if/then sentence.', frame: { kind: 'type', lines: [{ text: ifThen }] } },
    { title: 'Read the contrapositive', caption: 'Write the contrapositive in plain words.', frame: { kind: 'type', lines: [{ text: contraWords }] } },
  ] };
}

import { useEffect, useState } from 'react';
import { Check } from 'lucide-react';
import { drillDemos, type DemoFrame } from '../content/drillDemos';
import './drill-demo.css';

const STEP_MS = 5200;

function reducedMotion() {
  return typeof window !== 'undefined' && !!window.matchMedia?.('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches;
}

function Frame({ frame }: { frame: DemoFrame }) {
  if (frame.kind === 'pick') {
    let order = 0;
    return <div className="dd-pick">
      <p className="dd-sentence">{frame.words.map((word, index) => {
        const picked = frame.picks.includes(index);
        const delay = picked ? 0.7 + 0.7 * order++ : 0;
        return <span key={index} className={picked ? 'dd-word dd-picked' : 'dd-word'} style={picked ? { animationDelay: `${delay}s` } : undefined}>{word}</span>;
      })}</p>
      <span className="dd-chip" style={{ animationDelay: `${0.9 + 0.7 * frame.picks.length}s` }}>{frame.chip}</span>
    </div>;
  }
  if (frame.kind === 'type') return <div className="dd-type">{frame.lines.map((line, index) => <div key={index} className="dd-line">
    {line.label && <span className="dd-label">{line.label}</span>}
    <span className="dd-field"><span className="dd-typed" style={{ animationDelay: `${0.4 + index * 1.4}s` }}>{line.text}</span></span>
  </div>)}</div>;
  if (frame.kind === 'place') return <div className="dd-place">
    <div className="dd-slot"><span className="dd-label">Enough (left)</span><span className="dd-card dd-card-left">{frame.left}</span></div>
    <span className="dd-arrow" aria-hidden="true">→</span>
    <div className="dd-slot"><span className="dd-label">Required (right)</span><span className="dd-card dd-card-right">{frame.right}</span></div>
  </div>;
  return <div className="dd-choose">
    <p className="dd-question">{frame.question}</p>
    <div className="dd-options">{frame.options.map((option, index) => <span key={index} className={index === frame.correct ? 'dd-option dd-correct' : 'dd-option'}>
      <i>{index === frame.correct ? <Check size={13}/> : String.fromCharCode(65 + index)}</i>{option}
    </span>)}</div>
  </div>;
}

/** Instruction-page walkthrough: one animated frame per step of this drill, in drill order. */
export function DrillDemo({ drillId }: { drillId: string }) {
  const demo = drillDemos[drillId];
  const [index, setIndex] = useState(0);
  const [paused, setPaused] = useState(reducedMotion);
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!demo || paused) return;
    const timer = setTimeout(() => setIndex(current => (current + 1) % demo.steps.length), STEP_MS);
    return () => clearTimeout(timer);
  }, [demo, index, paused]);
  if (!demo) return null;
  const step = demo.steps[index];
  return <section className="dd-demo" aria-label="How this drill works">
    <p className="dd-eyebrow">How this drill works</p>
    {demo.sentence && <blockquote className="dd-example">Example: {demo.sentence}</blockquote>}
    <ol className="dd-steps">{demo.steps.map((item, i) => <li key={item.title}>
      <button type="button" aria-current={i === index ? 'step' : undefined} onClick={() => { setIndex(i); setPaused(true); }}><span>{i + 1}</span>{item.title}</button>
    </li>)}</ol>
    <div className="dd-stage" key={index} aria-live="polite">
      <p className="dd-caption"><strong>Step {index + 1}.</strong> {step.caption}</p>
      <Frame frame={step.frame}/>
    </div>
    {demo.steps.length > 1 && <div className="dd-progress" aria-hidden="true"><i key={`${index}-${paused}`} className={paused ? 'dd-bar-paused' : ''} style={{ animationDuration: `${STEP_MS}ms` }}/></div>}
  </section>;
}
====
.dd-demo{--dd-ink:#173544;--dd-soft:#5c7079;--dd-line:#d8d2c4;--dd-paper:#fffaf0;--dd-pick:#e9bd74;--dd-right:#1f7a54;--dd-right-bg:#e3f4ea;
  margin:22px 0 26px;padding:20px 22px 0;border:1px solid var(--dd-line);border-radius:14px;background:var(--dd-paper);color:var(--dd-ink);overflow:hidden;text-align:left}
.dd-eyebrow{margin:0 0 8px;font-size:11px;font-weight:700;letter-spacing:.15em;text-transform:uppercase;color:var(--dd-soft)}
.dd-example{margin:0 0 14px;padding:0;border:0;font:italic 17px/1.5 Georgia,serif;color:var(--dd-ink)}
.dd-steps{display:flex;flex-wrap:wrap;gap:6px;list-style:none;margin:0 0 16px;padding:0}
.dd-steps button{display:inline-flex;align-items:center;gap:7px;border:1px solid var(--dd-line);background:transparent;border-radius:999px;padding:4px 11px 4px 4px;font-size:13px;color:var(--dd-soft);cursor:pointer;transition:background .25s,color .25s,border-color .25s}
.dd-steps button span{display:grid;place-items:center;width:20px;height:20px;border-radius:50%;background:#efe9dc;font-size:11px;font-weight:700}
.dd-steps button[aria-current=step]{background:var(--dd-ink);border-color:var(--dd-ink);color:#fff}
.dd-steps button[aria-current=step] span{background:var(--dd-pick);color:var(--dd-ink)}
.dd-stage{min-height:150px;animation:dd-in .45s cubic-bezier(.2,.8,.2,1)}
.dd-caption{margin:0 0 14px;font-size:15px;line-height:1.5}
@keyframes dd-in{from{opacity:0;transform:translateX(16px)}to{opacity:1;transform:none}}

.dd-sentence{margin:0;font:clamp(19px,1.9vw,23px)/1.8 Georgia,serif}
.dd-word{display:inline-block;margin-right:.28em;padding:0 2px;border-radius:4px}
.dd-picked{animation:dd-pick .35s ease both}
@keyframes dd-pick{from{background:transparent;box-shadow:none}to{background:var(--dd-pick);box-shadow:0 0 0 2px var(--dd-pick)}}
.dd-chip{display:inline-block;margin-top:10px;font-size:14px;font-weight:700;color:var(--dd-right);background:var(--dd-right-bg);border:1px solid var(--dd-right);border-radius:999px;padding:3px 12px;animation:dd-rise .4s ease both}
@keyframes dd-rise{from{opacity:0;transform:translateY(6px)}to{opacity:1;transform:none}}

.dd-type{display:grid;gap:10px}
.dd-line{display:grid;gap:4px}
.dd-label{font-size:12px;font-weight:700;letter-spacing:.04em;color:var(--dd-soft)}
.dd-field{display:block;border:1px solid var(--dd-line);border-radius:8px;background:#fff;padding:9px 12px;min-height:42px;font-size:16px}
.dd-typed{display:inline-block;clip-path:inset(0 100% 0 0);animation:dd-type 1.2s steps(28,end) both}
@keyframes dd-type{to{clip-path:inset(0 0 0 0)}}

.dd-place{display:grid;grid-template-columns:1fr auto 1fr;align-items:end;gap:12px}
.dd-slot{display:grid;gap:6px}
.dd-card{display:block;min-height:46px;padding:11px 12px;border:1px dashed var(--dd-line);border-radius:8px;background:#fff;font-size:15px}
.dd-card-left{animation:dd-drop-left .6s .6s cubic-bezier(.2,.8,.2,1) both}
.dd-card-right{animation:dd-drop-right .6s 1.4s cubic-bezier(.2,.8,.2,1) both}
@keyframes dd-drop-left{from{opacity:0;transform:translate(-18px,-14px)}to{opacity:1;transform:none;border-style:solid;border-color:var(--dd-right)}}
@keyframes dd-drop-right{from{opacity:0;transform:translate(18px,-14px)}to{opacity:1;transform:none;border-style:solid;border-color:var(--dd-right)}}
.dd-arrow{font-size:22px;padding-bottom:10px;color:var(--dd-soft)}

.dd-question{margin:0 0 10px;font:17px/1.5 Georgia,serif}
.dd-options{display:grid;gap:8px}
.dd-option{display:flex;align-items:center;gap:10px;padding:9px 12px;border:1px solid var(--dd-line);border-radius:8px;background:#fff;font-size:15px}
.dd-option i{display:grid;place-items:center;width:22px;height:22px;border:1px solid var(--dd-line);border-radius:4px;font-style:normal;font-size:12px;color:var(--dd-soft)}
.dd-correct{animation:dd-right .4s 1.3s ease both}
.dd-correct i{animation:dd-right-mark .4s 1.3s ease both}
@keyframes dd-right{to{border-color:var(--dd-right);background:var(--dd-right-bg);box-shadow:inset 0 0 0 1px var(--dd-right)}}
@keyframes dd-right-mark{to{background:var(--dd-right);border-color:var(--dd-right);color:#fff}}

.dd-progress{height:3px;margin:16px -22px 0;background:#efe9dc}
.dd-progress i{display:block;height:100%;width:100%;background:var(--dd-pick);transform-origin:left;animation:dd-bar linear both}
.dd-progress i.dd-bar-paused{animation:none;transform:scaleX(0)}
@keyframes dd-bar{from{transform:scaleX(0)}to{transform:scaleX(1)}}

@media(max-width:600px){.dd-place{grid-template-columns:1fr}.dd-arrow{display:none}}
@media(prefers-reduced-motion:reduce){.dd-stage,.dd-picked,.dd-chip,.dd-typed,.dd-card-left,.dd-card-right,.dd-correct,.dd-correct i{animation-duration:1ms!important;animation-delay:0s!important}}
.dark .dd-demo{--dd-ink:#edf4ed;--dd-soft:#b5c9cc;--dd-line:#4f666d;--dd-paper:#1e3741;--dd-right-bg:#1d4a38;--dd-right:#5fc495}
.dark .dd-field,.dark .dd-card,.dark .dd-option{background:#152b36}.dark .dd-picked{color:#173544}

/* Transform-only connector motion, with the same static reading. */
@keyframes cr-travel {
  0% { transform: translateX(0); opacity: 0; }
  25% { opacity: 1; }
  100% { transform: translateX(1.5rem); opacity: 0; }
}
@keyframes cr-travel-vertical {
  0% { transform: translateY(0); opacity: 0; }
  25% { opacity: 1; }
  100% { transform: translateY(1.5rem); opacity: 0; }
}

  const firstChoice = useRef<HTMLButtonElement>(null);
  return <div className="cr-question co-check"><h3 id={id}>{prompt}</h3><div className="cr-answer-options" role="group" aria-labelledby={id}>
    {options.map((option, index) => <button ref={index === 0 ? firstChoice : undefined} type="button" key={option} aria-pressed={choice === index} onClick={() => { setChoice(index); onAttempt?.(index); }}>{option}</button>)}
  </div><div role="status" aria-live="polite">{choice !== null && <div className={`cr-feedback ${choice === correct ? 'cr-correct' : 'cr-revise'}`}><strong>{choice === correct ? 'Correct.' : 'Not quite.'}</strong><p>{feedback[choice]}</p></div>}</div>

function HypotheticalFigure() {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLElement>(null);
  const [inView, setInView] = useState(false);
  useEffect(() => {
    if (!ref.current || typeof IntersectionObserver === 'undefined') return;
    const observer = new IntersectionObserver(([entry]) => setInView(entry.isIntersecting), { threshold: .35 });
    observer.observe(ref.current);
    return () => observer.disconnect();
  }, []);
  return <figure className="cr-hypothetical co-if-connection" ref={ref} data-in-view={inView}>
    <div className="cr-if"><span>IF</span><strong>this condition is true</strong><small>A case to imagine</small></div>
    <div className="cr-connection" aria-hidden="true"><i/><i/><i/></div>
    <div className="cr-then"><span>THEN</span><strong>the other condition has to be true</strong><small>What the rule proves</small></div>
    <figcaption>If the first condition is true, the other must be true. Imagining that case doesn't tell us it happened.</figcaption>
  </figure>;
}

type FamilyEntry = { word: string; sentence: string; explanation: string };
const sufficientFamily: FamilyEntry[] = [
  { word: 'if', sentence: 'If someone borrows a locker, they have a key.', explanation: 'Borrowing a locker is enough to prove that the person has a key.' },
  { word: 'when / whenever', sentence: 'Whenever someone borrows a locker, they have a key.', explanation: 'Whenever covers every occasion of borrowing. A report that something happened once when someone borrowed a locker would not prove this general rule.' },
  { word: 'any / anytime', sentence: 'Any person who borrows a locker has a key.', explanation: 'Pick any person meeting the borrowing condition. The rule gives that person a key. Anytime someone borrows a locker, they have a key expresses the same connection across occasions.' },
  { word: 'all', sentence: 'All people who borrow a locker have a key.', explanation: 'All covers every person in the described group. Keep who borrow a locker with people; the rule is not about everyone.' },
  { word: 'every / every time', sentence: 'Every person who borrows a locker has a key.', explanation: 'Every member of this group meets the other condition. Every time someone borrows a locker, they have a key keeps the rule while speaking about occasions.' },
  { word: 'in order to', sentence: 'In order to borrow a locker, a person needs a key.', explanation: 'Borrowing the locker is the goal. Having a key is its requirement. This does not say that having a key is enough to borrow a locker.' },
  { word: 'people who', sentence: 'People who borrow a locker have a key.', explanation: 'People who borrow a locker names the complete group being described. For a person in that group, having a key follows.' },
  { word: 'each', sentence: 'Each person who borrows a locker has a key.', explanation: 'Each applies the statement to every individual locker borrower. We have changed the wording, not what borrowing proves.' },
];
const necessaryFamily: FamilyEntry[] = [
  { word: 'then', sentence: 'If someone borrows a locker, then they have a key.', explanation: 'Then introduces what the borrowing condition proves: the person has a key.' },
  { word: 'must', sentence: 'A person borrowing a locker must have a key.', explanation: 'Here we are describing a requirement under the stated rule. If must appears in an instruction instead, keep that meaning: a required action is not proof that someone completed it.' },
  { word: 'necessary', sentence: 'Having a key is necessary for borrowing a locker.', explanation: 'Ask necessary for what? For borrowing a locker. Having a key is the requirement, not a promise that someone borrowed.' },
  { word: 'required', sentence: 'Having a key is required for borrowing a locker.', explanation: 'Required names the same rule. Every locker borrower meets the key requirement.' },
  { word: 'only / only if', sentence: 'A person borrows a locker only if they have a key.', explanation: 'Only if makes having a key necessary. Only people with a key borrow a locker says the same thing. Neither gives a locker to every person with a key.' },
  { word: 'depends', sentence: 'Borrowing a locker depends on having a key.', explanation: 'Having a key is what the borrowing depends on. The rule does not make the key enough by itself.' },
  { word: 'need / need to', sentence: 'A person needs a key to borrow a locker.', explanation: 'What does the person need? A key. A person needs to have a key to borrow a locker expresses the same requirement.' },
  { word: 'have to', sentence: 'A person has to have a key to borrow a locker.', explanation: 'Having a key is required for borrowing. Read have to have a key as one complete requirement.' },
  { word: 'essential', sentence: 'Having a key is essential for borrowing a locker.', explanation: 'Essential for borrowing names the requirement. The borrowing cannot meet this rule without the key.' },
  { word: 'precondition', sentence: 'Having a key is a precondition for borrowing a locker.', explanation: 'A precondition is something the other condition requires. Do not infer that the requirement was sufficient, or invent a timeline from the label.' },
];

export function WordFamily({ entries, role }: { entries: FamilyEntry[]; role: 'sufficient' | 'necessary' }) {

## Active-teaching comparison and pending round

- Intro to LR source: IntroLessonPage.tsx mounts QuestionAnatomyScanner on the three-parts note and SupportSimulator on so-an-argument. Scanner uses staged selection/highlighting and explanatory reveals; simulator assembles support under a conclusion. Findings are source-verified, not live browser acceptance.
- Browser attempt: known Intro three-parts route redirected to auth on localhost8097. No signed-in Intro interaction proof obtained. Current sufficient tab retained; no design implementation in this interview.
- Q1 pending: make the student-group visual interactive, with selecting a given fact highlighting what is certain and what remains possible? Recommendation yes. === Mastering Conditional Statements.md
10036 Mastering Conditional Statements.md
A misunderstanding of conditional statements is the most common source of error on the LSAT. Conditional statements are at the heart of logical reasoning. They’re also at the heart of how human beings make arguments in regular, day-to-day discussions. Here are some examples of conditional statements at work in daily life:

- If I increase my LSAT score by a handful of points, I’ll be able to go to law school for free.
- I want to go to bed early tonight; I don’t want to be too tired tomorrow morning.
- If I fail this exam it’ll hurt my GPA.
- He agreed to get us a table if he arrives at the restaurant first.

If you’ve never studied formal logic, or if you hate it with a passion, don’t worry. Conditional statements are common sense. You just don’t know it yet.

![](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RMAzOPvOHLw)

## What Is a Conditional Statement?

A conditional statement is just an if-then statement like this one:

`**If** you drink that poison, **then** you will get sick.`

The if-clause is called the “sufficient condition,” and the then-clause is called the “necessary condition.” If nothing else sticks from this lesson, remember this distinction.

## What Is a Sufficient Condition?

LSAT test-writers refer to the if-clause as the “sufficient condition.”

It’s sufficient because it’s enough to guarantee that the then-clause will happen. You don’t need anything else. If you drink that poison, then, according to this rule, you will get sick.

In 
=== Dashboard.md

Transcript

Sets, members, and the relationships between them are abstract.

Talking about them forces us to use **some language**. Up to this point, we've been talking about them in **English**, a natural language, supplemented with a **visual language** of circles and dots. I already mentioned that English isn't particularly great at [highlighting the form of the argument](https://7sage.com/lessons/slug/the-conditional-argument). Yet, in formal arguments, the most important thing about the argument is its form.

The visual language is helpful and whenever you can use it, I encourage you to. But there are at least two reasons why we cannot rely solely on it. One reason is that conditional logic doesn't always map onto set logic. For example, sufficiency and necessity relationships won't always be about tangible objects that you can intuitively visualize into set circles. Another reason is that the visual language is cumbersome. It's not nimble enough for us to do manipulations to reveal what's missing in order to answer the questions. That might not make sense yet, but that's okay. Push on and come back to this lesson later. I promise it will make sense.

We'll need to learn another language specifically designed to shine a spotlight on the most important features of formal arguments. That language is called " **lawgic**." Gag, pun, I know. But, I've made up my mind and that's what we're calling it. If you don't like it, sue me. But first, you ha

2304 README.md
# Inbox

The one intake queue. Everything unprocessed lands here and nothing else does.

This is the intended destination for Obsidian Web Clipper templates. The browser's
selected template controls its actual save location; verify that it names this
vault and `raw/inbox/`. Some recent clips still exist in the legacy top-level
`Clippings/` folder. Downloads and other one-off sources can be dropped here too.

## The contract

A file sitting here is an intake candidate. Its presence alone does not establish
whether it has already been processed, because preserved originals remain here.

When Joshua asks to ingest a clip, `ingest` checks its content hash and prior
outputs, copies and hash-verifies it into `raw/sources/clips/<slug>/` with
`PROVENANCE.md` and `SHA256SUMS`, reads it, and compiles useful content into the
existing owning pages in wiki, corpus, references, context or student records.
It updates the source summary, indexes and `wiki/log.md`, checks links and
contradictions, and reports gaps. It does not move or delete the original by
default. Copying a clip here does not itself run ingestion.

Processed status comes from hashes, linked source summaries and ingest log
entries, not whether the inbox is empty. A duplicate already compiled source
does not need another knowledge page. See `system/schema/llm-wiki.md` and the
active `ingest` skill for the governing workflow.

## assistant

## JOB 1: Voice

**One finding first.** There are no exclamation marks in any Main Conclusion lesson copy. I grepped the whole folder. The only ones are in app feedback strings:
- "Correct!" (`modules/lessons/EmbeddedDrillRunner.tsx:378`)
- "Correct — you named it!" (`modules/drills/labs/ConclusionTypeLab.tsx:363`)
- "Perfect dissection!" (`components/structured-dissection/StructuredDissectionLab.tsx:123`)

Most lesson shells are neutral and declarative. The warm, personable voice is concentrated in `IntroLessonPage.tsx` (the premises, conclusions and so-an-argument pages) and in the phased-lesson briefings. I can't tell which lines Joshua wrote and which were drafted for him. Treat the energy below as "plain with a wink", not "excitable".

Base: `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os-projects\lsatu-conditional-bootcamp\src\bootcamps\main-conclusion\`
- IL = `modules\lessons\notes\IntroLessonPage.tsx`
- LP = `content\lessons-phased.source.ts`

1. "We're going to learn to manipulate them, dismantle them, and beat them." (IL:405)
2. "But first, you need a crystal clear picture of what an argument actually is." (IL:405-406)
3. "Arguments are quite simple at their core." (IL:413)
4. "That's it. Arguments are just premises and conclusions." (IL:428-429)
5. "Premises are just the facts. The evidence." (IL:461)
6. "They describe how the world is, and on the LSAT, you accept them as true, full stop." (IL:462)
7. "In real life, you'd attack the facts they used to support the conclusion." (IL:471)
8. "If you catch yourself doubting the evidence, stop." (IL:478)
9. "That's the whole game." (IL:490)
10. "They just exist, solid, already true, handed to you for free." (IL:502)
11. "That's a harder, more useful question, and it's the one that gets you points." (IL:508)
12. "FABS. Memorize it." (IL:526)
13. "Here's the thing, though. Indicator words are a tool, not a crutch." (IL:628)
14. "They won't always be there. If your entire strategy is seek-and-find, you'll be stuck." (IL:634)
15. "Indicator words will speed you up when they're present. Analytical thinking will save you when they're not." (IL:646)
16. "The argument did not change. Only the packaging did." (IL:666)
17. "Finding the main conclusion sounds easy until the LSAT gives you five sentences that all feel important." (IL:883)
18. "And the real conclusion? It is usually hiding in the middle of the wreckage, camouflaged by everything around it." (IL:891)
19. "That is why you need a definitive check." (IL:902)
20. "Two questions. Both must be yes. No exceptions." (IL:932)
21. "Do not ask what the sentence says. Ask what job it is doing." (IL:218-219)
22. "Before we hunt the conclusion, we have to know what kind of beast we are hunting." (LP:163)
23. "Premise. The silliest one. Accept it anyway. Premises are not where the action is." (LP:231)
24. "School taught you the conclusion comes last. The LSAT writers know that." (LP:546)
25. "A concession sounds agreeable. That is the trap. Once the concession ends, the author swings." (LP:1034)

Two extras: "They are coats and scarves; you can hang them up at the door." (`content\hard-sentences.source.ts:92`), and "Your first question is not, 'Which answer looks good?'" (`modules\lessons\notes\argument-basics.tsx:89`).

**Recurring moves**
- **Opening a concept:** a plain claim, then a contrast with real life or school. "In real life we attack the facts" comes before "never on the LSAT". "School taught you the conclusion comes last" comes before the real rule.
- **Landing a key point:** very short fragments stacked one after another ("Two questions. Both must be yes. No exceptions."). A bold one-liner closes the paragraph ("FABS. Memorize it.").
- **Humor:** dry and quiet. Metaphors ("beast", "wreckage", "coats and scarves", "wind-up and swing"), deadpan silliness ("The silliest one. Accept it anyway."), and the parenthetical "(silly)".
- **Direct address:** heavy "you" with contractions ("you'd", "won't", "that's"). A rhetorical question he answers himself ("And the real conclusion?"). "Here's the thing, though."
- **Exclamation marks:** none in teaching copy. Reserve them for correct-answer feedback only, and sparingly. This is my reading, not a stated rule.

**Avoid**
- Em dashes. `lessons-phased.source.ts` uses them constantly, so do not imitate it there.
- Label headings like "Student Takeaway", "Key Rule", "Primary Directive", "Structural Triad" and "Later-language note". They read as internal production metadata.
- "Door" as a unit of content.
- The "it's not just X, it's Y" shape, and the spec-voice phrases "the stimulus is the content you are being asked about" and "source-anchored".

## JOB 2: Visual ideas

Everything here comes from reading source and brainstorm files. I haven't run or seen any of it in a browser, so all motion behavior is **unverified**. The inbox clips are mostly AI-tooling videos with little on teaching-visual design. The useful ones are the Demon "Abstract Conditionals" transcript (contrapositive as common sense: Boston/Massachusetts, and "if you smoke you are not healthy" as a reverse trap) and the "Claude Now Does Video" clip (3Blue1Brown-style explainer, 0:57).

Brainstorm constraints worth honoring:
- `2026-10-09-sufficient-condition-lesson.md`: motion must carry the reasoning, with keyboard and reduced-motion alternatives.
- `2026-09-23-classroom-home-visual-upgrade.md`: gentle pulse, all motion off under reduced-motion.
- `2026-10-11-conditional-indicator-flashcards.md`: shake the card only, not the page (tentative).

### (a) What already exists (all under `src\bootcamps\conditionals\`)

- **`components\DrillDemo.tsx` + `drill-demo.css` + `content\drillDemos.ts`:**
  - An auto-advancing stepper (5.2s per step) with a progress bar and reduced-motion handling.
  - Frame types are `pick`, `type`, `place` and `choose`.
  - Keyframes include `dd-pick` (staggered highlight), `dd-type` (typewriter via clip-path), `dd-drop-left/right` (cards drop into slots) and `dd-right`.
  - A `contrapositiveDemo()` builder already exists. Its "Reverse and negate" step is plain typed lines.
- **`components\ConditionalReferenceSequence.tsx` (`HypotheticalFigure`):**
  - An IntersectionObserver sets `data-in-view`.
  - CSS in `opening.css:82-87`, `reference-sequence.css:55-58` and `opening-polish.css:332` provides `co-emphasize-if` (highlight), `co-connect-conditions` (thread grows) and `cr-travel` (dot moves along the connector).
- **`components\must-arrow-note.css` + `OpeningCourseContent.tsx:249-253`:** an IF-card, arrow and THEN-card grid, with `ma-follow-arrow` nudging the SVG arrow.
- **`components\SufficientConditionExperience.tsx` + `sufficient-condition-experience.css`:**
  - `sc-membership[data-case]` SVG states with 240ms fill/stroke transitions.
  - A toggle row (`sc-case-controls`).
  - The `SufficientReasoningWalkthrough` rule/fact/inference sequence.
  - `nc-required-emphasis` (pulsing background glow) and `sc-guarantee-emphasis`.
- **`components\CounterexampleLab.tsx`:** `NegativeCaseLab` room toggles and a ticket builder (`co-ticket[data-blue]`). The lit-room drawing (`lesson-pages.css:642-675`, `i[data-lit=true]` with a glow box-shadow) is the closest existing match to a light bulb.
- **`components\indicator-word-deck.css`:** a 3D flip card (`iw-card` rotateY) and `iw-shake` for wrong answers.
- **Also reusable:**
  - `oc-start-pulse` ring (`opening-course.css:116-121`).
  - `sr-pop`/`sr-shake` (`staged-reasoning-drill.css:23`).
  - `.mc-shine` sweep (`main-conclusion\styles\scoped.css:145-190`).
  - `framer-motion` is already a dependency (`package.json:99`), so `layout` animation needs no new library.

### (b) Contrapositive ideas

1. **Bulb board with four situations.** Power on/off and light on/off toggles drive a glowing bulb. The "light on, no power" cell shakes and is stamped "can't happen". Extend `co-room-drawing[data-lit]` and the `sc-membership[data-case]` pattern, with `iw-shake` for the forbidden case.
2. **Sides swap, then "NOT" pops on.** Two clicks: the cards trade places, then NOT chips scale in on both. Extend the `ma-relationship` grid. The swap can use framer-motion `layout`, or a hand-computed translate. The pop reuses `sr-pop` or `dd-rise`.
3. **Punch-in on "must".** The key word scales to about 1.1 and back, with a highlight. Reuse `nc-required-emphasis` or `co-emphasize-if`, triggered by `HypotheticalFigure`'s `data-in-view` observer. Run it once, not on a loop.
4. **Thread redraws backwards.** The original draws power to light. The contrapositive draws a fresh thread from "no light" to "no power", with the `cr-travel` dot riding it. Extend `co-connect-conditions` and `cr-travel`.
5. **Twin pulse.** Original and contrapositive sit side by side with an "=" badge. Focusing one rings both in sync. Reuse `oc-start-pulse` plus `nc-required-emphasis`, limited to a few iterations.
6. **Tempting-but-wrong tray.** Reverse and inverse versions each get tried and fail with a shake. The contrapositive passes with the green `dd-right` treatment. Extend `RuleComparisonLab` and the `CounterexampleLab` ticket builder ("build a case that breaks it").
7. **Contrapositive "film".** Add a contrapositive entry to `drillDemos.ts` with a new `swap` frame kind in `DrillDemo.tsx`. It reuses the existing stepper, timing and reduced-motion handling. It also upgrades the plain typed "Reverse and negate" step in `contrapositiveDemo()`.
8. **Cut-the-power button.** One button fades the bulb out over about 240ms (same transition as `sc-law-boundary`), then "So the light is not on" types in with `dd-typed`.
9. **Rule, fact, inference chips.** The student drops a "no power" fact chip into the arrow slot. It locks in (`dd-card-left`), then the inference chip rises (`dd-rise`). Extend `SufficientReasoningWalkthrough`.
10. **Flip-card reflex deck.** Front: "No power." Back: "So the light is off." Wrong answers shake the card only, per the flashcard brainstorm. Extend `IndicatorWordDeck`/`FlashcardTools` and the `iw-card` flip.
11. **Circles view.** The Light circle sits inside the Power circle. Toggling "outside Power" shades everything outside both circles. Extend the `sc-membership` SVG and its fill transitions.
12. **Staggered reveal on scroll.** Each step of "flip it, then negate both" appears in sequence using `animationDelay` steps like `dd-pick` (0.7s each), starting when the figure enters view.

**Unverified:** I haven't opened the lesson page that currently teaches the contrapositive. The files that mention it are `ConditionalReferenceSequence.tsx`, `OpeningCourseContent.tsx` and `CounterexampleLab.tsx`, but I haven't read their contrapositive copy. I also haven't checked cross-browser behavior of the clip-path typewriter or the rotateY flip, or mobile layout of any new swap animation.
